People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um… thank you for seeing me. I guess the main thing is my sleep. It’s probably not the worst thing, but it’s been going on for a long time now. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say preoccupied, no. I mean, those things cross my mind like anyone’s, especially work stuff or bills, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying about them all day. At least, I don’t think so… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or sluggish during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get through my day, but I do feel pretty worn out a lot. I mean… if I do get a full night, which isn’t always, I still tend to wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. If things are actually going okay, I’m usually not sitting there looking for something to worry about. I might still feel kind of on edge or like I’m waiting for the next thing sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it worrying exactly. Is that helpful?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your worries are excessive compared to what's actually going on in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / home_health_aide / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF51.0, G47.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um… thank you for seeing me. I guess the main thing is my sleep. It’s probably not the worst thing, but it’s been going on for a long time now. I go to bed just exhausted, and then I wake up at every little sound in the house. And then I’m awake before the alarm, feeling like I didn’t really rest at all. I wasn’t sure if that’s something to come in for, but it’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly… I mean, I have normal worries, I guess, like anyone. I don’t think I’m sitting there panicking or anything. Maybe a little keyed up sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s enough to count.\n\nDoctor: Do you have bad dreams or nightmares?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no… at least not that I’d call bad dreams. It’s more that my sleep is just broken. I fall into bed so tired, but then every little noise wakes me up, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than I should, anyway. I mean, I worry about regular things, but I wouldn’t say it takes over or anything like that. Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worries?\n\nPatient: I think I can usually control them… or, at least, I don’t feel like they’re running away with me. I might turn something over in my head for a bit, but I can usually tell myself, you know, “that’s enough,” and get on with what I need to do. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say preoccupied, no. I mean, those things cross my mind like anyone’s, especially work stuff or bills, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying about them all day. At least, I don’t think so… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or sluggish during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get through my day, but I do feel pretty worn out a lot. By the time I’m done seeing my home-health clients and then come back and help with things at home, my body just feels heavy. Even doing my charting notes or folding laundry can feel like a lot to push through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel rested after a full night of uninterrupted sleep, if that happens?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… if I do get a full night, which isn’t always, I still tend to wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. I fall into bed tired, but I wake up at little noises in the house, and then I’m often awake before the alarm. It’s not terrible every single night, but it’s been that way for a long time. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. If things are actually going okay, I’m usually not sitting there looking for something to worry about. I might still feel kind of on edge or like I’m waiting for the next thing sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it worrying exactly. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are excessive compared to what's actually going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re excessive. I mean, I have normal things to think about, like work and home stuff, but I don’t usually feel like I’m blowing things way out of proportion. At least… I don’t think so. Maybe I’m not the best judge, but it doesn’t feel like constant worrying for no reason. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out or on your mind when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no. I mean, sometimes the usual house or work things are on my mind, but it’s more that my sleep is just… broken. I’ll fall into bed exhausted, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. And then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Is your sleep usually worse on nights when you've had a particularly stressful day?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but honestly it’s kind of like that most nights, even when the day wasn’t especially awful. I’ll be so tired and think I’m going to sleep hard, but then I wake up at every little sound in the house, and I’m up before the alarm anyway. It just doesn’t feel like I really rested. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake in bed thinking about something in particular when you can't fall back asleep after waking up to a noise?\n\nPatient: Not usually anything in particular, no. I mean, sometimes my mind wanders a little, but it’s more like… I wake up from some small noise, and then I just can’t really settle back in. I’m tired, I want to sleep, but it feels broken up, and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I wasn’t sure if that’s something to come in for, but it’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly… I mean, I have normal worries, I guess, like anyone. I don’t think I’m sitting there panicking or anything. Maybe a little keyed up sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s enough to count.\n\nDoctor: Do you have bad dreams or nightmares?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no… at least not that I’d call bad dreams. It’s more that my sleep is just broken. I fall into bed so tired, but then every little noise wakes me up, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than I should, anyway. I mean, I worry about regular things, but I wouldn’t say it takes over or anything like that. Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worries?\n\nPatient: I think I can usually control them… or, at least, I don’t feel like they’re running away with me. I might turn something over in my head for a bit, but I can usually tell myself, you know, “that’s enough,” and get on with what I need to do. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say preoccupied, no. I mean, those things cross my mind like anyone’s, especially work stuff or bills, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying about them all day. At least, I don’t think so… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or sluggish during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get through my day, but I do feel pretty worn out a lot. By the time I’m done seeing my home-health clients and then come back and help with things at home, my body just feels heavy. Even doing my charting notes or folding laundry can feel like a lot to push through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel rested after a full night of uninterrupted sleep, if that happens?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… if I do get a full night, which isn’t always, I still tend to wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. I fall into bed tired, but I wake up at little noises in the house, and then I’m often awake before the alarm. It’s not terrible every single night, but it’s been that way for a long time. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. If things are actually going okay, I’m usually not sitting there looking for something to worry about. I might still feel kind of on edge or like I’m waiting for the next thing sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it worrying exactly. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are excessive compared to what's actually going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re excessive. I mean, I have normal things to think about, like work and home stuff, but I don’t usually feel like I’m blowing things way out of proportion. At least… I don’t think so. Maybe I’m not the best judge, but it doesn’t feel like constant worrying for no reason. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out or on your mind when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no. I mean, sometimes the usual house or work things are on my mind, but it’s more that my sleep is just… broken. I’ll fall into bed exhausted, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. And then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Is your sleep usually worse on nights when you've had a particularly stressful day?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but honestly it’s kind of like that most nights, even when the day wasn’t especially awful. I’ll be so tired and think I’m going to sleep hard, but then I wake up at every little sound in the house, and I’m up before the alarm anyway. It just doesn’t feel like I really rested. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake in bed thinking about something in particular when you can't fall back asleep after waking up to a noise?\n\nPatient: Not usually anything in particular, no. I mean, sometimes my mind wanders a little, but it’s more like… I wake up from some small noise, and then I just can’t really settle back in. I’m tired, I want to sleep, but it feels broken up, and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are related to your overall mood or emotional state?\n\nPatient: I guess it probably is, yes. I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but most days things just feel kind of… flat, like the color’s gone out of them. Even when I’m with my family at Sunday dinner, or looking at art I used to really enjoy, I’m just not feeling it the same way. And sometimes I have to work pretty hard not to tear up, even when there isn’t any clear reason. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sleep problems started around the same time as you began feeling this flat, unmotivated mood?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean, it’s hard to pin it down exactly, but it feels like this all sort of started after my mother-in-law moved in with us… a little over two years ago. Not that I’m blaming her, I don’t want it to sound like that, but that’s about when I started noticing it. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I wasn’t sure if that’s something to come in for, but it’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly… I mean, I have normal worries, I guess, like anyone. I don’t think I’m sitting there panicking or anything. Maybe a little keyed up sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s enough to count.\n\nDoctor: Do you have bad dreams or nightmares?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no… at least not that I’d call bad dreams. It’s more that my sleep is just broken. I fall into bed so tired, but then every little noise wakes me up, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not more than I should, anyway. I mean, I worry about regular things, but I wouldn’t say it takes over or anything like that. Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control your worries?\n\nPatient: I think I can usually control them… or, at least, I don’t feel like they’re running away with me. I might turn something over in my head for a bit, but I can usually tell myself, you know, “that’s enough,” and get on with what I need to do. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say preoccupied, no. I mean, those things cross my mind like anyone’s, especially work stuff or bills, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying about them all day. At least, I don’t think so… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or sluggish during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get through my day, but I do feel pretty worn out a lot. By the time I’m done seeing my home-health clients and then come back and help with things at home, my body just feels heavy. Even doing my charting notes or folding laundry can feel like a lot to push through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel rested after a full night of uninterrupted sleep, if that happens?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… if I do get a full night, which isn’t always, I still tend to wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. I fall into bed tired, but I wake up at little noises in the house, and then I’m often awake before the alarm. It’s not terrible every single night, but it’s been that way for a long time. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. If things are actually going okay, I’m usually not sitting there looking for something to worry about. I might still feel kind of on edge or like I’m waiting for the next thing sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it worrying exactly. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are excessive compared to what's actually going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re excessive. I mean, I have normal things to think about, like work and home stuff, but I don’t usually feel like I’m blowing things way out of proportion. At least… I don’t think so. Maybe I’m not the best judge, but it doesn’t feel like constant worrying for no reason. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out or on your mind when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no. I mean, sometimes the usual house or work things are on my mind, but it’s more that my sleep is just… broken. I’ll fall into bed exhausted, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. And then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Is your sleep usually worse on nights when you've had a particularly stressful day?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but honestly it’s kind of like that most nights, even when the day wasn’t especially awful. I’ll be so tired and think I’m going to sleep hard, but then I wake up at every little sound in the house, and I’m up before the alarm anyway. It just doesn’t feel like I really rested. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake in bed thinking about something in particular when you can't fall back asleep after waking up to a noise?\n\nPatient: Not usually anything in particular, no. I mean, sometimes my mind wanders a little, but it’s more like… I wake up from some small noise, and then I just can’t really settle back in. I’m tired, I want to sleep, but it feels broken up, and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are related to your overall mood or emotional state?\n\nPatient: I guess it probably is, yes. I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but most days things just feel kind of… flat, like the color’s gone out of them. Even when I’m with my family at Sunday dinner, or looking at art I used to really enjoy, I’m just not feeling it the same way. And sometimes I have to work pretty hard not to tear up, even when there isn’t any clear reason. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sleep problems started around the same time as you began feeling this flat, unmotivated mood?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean, it’s hard to pin it down exactly, but it feels like this all sort of started after my mother-in-law moved in with us… a little over two years ago. Not that I’m blaming her, I don’t want it to sound like that, but that’s about when I started noticing it. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I enjoy nothing, exactly, but things don’t really feel the way they used to. Like Sunday dinner with my family, or looking at art I used to love—it’s like the color has gone out of it a bit. And sometimes I’m trying not to tear up, even when I couldn’t tell you why. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I mean, I still go to Sunday dinner with my family, and I still look at art sometimes because I know I used to love it. It’s not that I hate those things now… it’s just harder to really feel the enjoyment. Most days it’s like the color’s gone out of things, and sometimes I’m sitting there trying not to tear up and I don’t even know why. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I wouldn’t say worthless, exactly. That sounds a little strong. I do feel bad sometimes, like I should be handling things better or doing more than I am, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by guilt. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "Determine the presence of persistent and pervasive feelings of worthlessness or guilt", "question": "Do you often feel like you're a bad person or that you've failed in some important way?"}

I might turn something over in my head for a bit, but I can usually tell myself, you know, “that’s enough,” and get on with what I need to do. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with worries about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say preoccupied, no. I mean, those things cross my mind like anyone’s, especially work stuff or bills, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying about them all day. At least, I don’t think so… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or sluggish during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I do get through my day, but I do feel pretty worn out a lot. By the time I’m done seeing my home-health clients and then come back and help with things at home, my body just feels heavy. Even doing my charting notes or folding laundry can feel like a lot to push through. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel rested after a full night of uninterrupted sleep, if that happens?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean… if I do get a full night, which isn’t always, I still tend to wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. I fall into bed tired, but I wake up at little noises in the house, and then I’m often awake before the alarm. It’s not terrible every single night, but it’s been that way for a long time. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when everything is going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way. If things are actually going okay, I’m usually not sitting there looking for something to worry about. I might still feel kind of on edge or like I’m waiting for the next thing sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it worrying exactly. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are excessive compared to what's actually going on in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’re excessive. I mean, I have normal things to think about, like work and home stuff, but I don’t usually feel like I’m blowing things way out of proportion. At least… I don’t think so. Maybe I’m not the best judge, but it doesn’t feel like constant worrying for no reason. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out or on your mind when you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Not really one specific thing, no. I mean, sometimes the usual house or work things are on my mind, but it’s more that my sleep is just… broken. I’ll fall into bed exhausted, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. And then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Is your sleep usually worse on nights when you've had a particularly stressful day?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but honestly it’s kind of like that most nights, even when the day wasn’t especially awful. I’ll be so tired and think I’m going to sleep hard, but then I wake up at every little sound in the house, and I’m up before the alarm anyway. It just doesn’t feel like I really rested. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake in bed thinking about something in particular when you can't fall back asleep after waking up to a noise?\n\nPatient: Not usually anything in particular, no. I mean, sometimes my mind wanders a little, but it’s more like… I wake up from some small noise, and then I just can’t really settle back in. I’m tired, I want to sleep, but it feels broken up, and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are related to your overall mood or emotional state?\n\nPatient: I guess it probably is, yes. I don’t want to make it sound too dramatic, but most days things just feel kind of… flat, like the color’s gone out of them. Even when I’m with my family at Sunday dinner, or looking at art I used to really enjoy, I’m just not feeling it the same way. And sometimes I have to work pretty hard not to tear up, even when there isn’t any clear reason. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sleep problems started around the same time as you began feeling this flat, unmotivated mood?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean, it’s hard to pin it down exactly, but it feels like this all sort of started after my mother-in-law moved in with us… a little over two years ago. Not that I’m blaming her, I don’t want it to sound like that, but that’s about when I started noticing it. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I enjoy nothing, exactly, but things don’t really feel the way they used to. Like Sunday dinner with my family, or looking at art I used to love—it’s like the color has gone out of it a bit. And sometimes I’m trying not to tear up, even when I couldn’t tell you why. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I mean, I still go to Sunday dinner with my family, and I still look at art sometimes because I know I used to love it. It’s not that I hate those things now… it’s just harder to really feel the enjoyment. Most days it’s like the color’s gone out of things, and sometimes I’m sitting there trying not to tear up and I don’t even know why. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I wouldn’t say worthless, exactly. That sounds a little strong. I do feel bad sometimes, like I should be handling things better or doing more than I am, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by guilt. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're a bad person or that you've failed in some important way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m a bad person, no. Failed… I mean, sometimes I feel like I should’ve done more, or handled things better, but I don’t know if I’d call that failing in an important way. Maybe I’m being too careful with the wording. I just don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
